Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
The notes say: *"Odometry error is unbounded: each step adds error that is never removed, so position error grows with distance travelled. Heading error is the worst kind because a heading error of e radians becomes a position error of about d \* e after a further distance d."*

In plain words, there are two claims. First, odometry only ever adds the next small step to its current estimate, and nothing in the method checks that estimate against the world. Any error in one step stays in every later estimate, and new errors keep piling on. Second, of all the errors that can pile up, an error in heading `theta` does the most harm, because it keeps creating new position error for as long as the robot keeps driving.

**Why heading is different, using 4.2.** Look at the pose update in 4.2:

- `x += dS * cos(theta + dtheta/2)`
- `y += dS * sin(theta + dtheta/2)`
- `theta += dtheta`

`theta` is not just one of the outputs. It goes into every later `x` and `y` update, because it sets which way each step of length `dS` is pointed. Compare two kinds of mistake:

1. **A wrong `dS` in one step** (say the wheel travelled slightly further than the encoder counted). The position is off by that small amount, and that error just stays. It doesn't make later steps worse. (My reading: the notes don't make this comparison directly. I'm drawing it from the update equations.)
2. **A wrong `theta`, off by `e`.** Because `theta += dtheta` only adds and never corrects, the error `e` stays in `theta` from then on. Every later step goes into the cos/sin with the wrong angle, so every step points slightly off course. After a further distance `d`, all those slightly-off steps add up to a sideways miss of about `d * e`. That's the 4.3 formula.

So a distance error is a one-off. A heading error keeps growing in proportion to how far you drive afterwards.

**Where heading error comes from (linking to 4.2 and 4.4).** `dtheta = (dS_R - dS_L) / L` depends on the *difference* between the two wheels, divided by the wheelbase `L`. My reading is that this is why the 4.4 sources matter so much. Unequal wheel radii make `dS_R` and `dS_L` slightly wrong relative to each other, and a wrong `L` scales every `dtheta`. Both feed straight into heading, and from heading into position.

**Gaps in the notes.**

- `from outside the material:` The "about" in "about `d * e`" comes from the small-angle approximation. The true sideways miss is `d * sin(e)`, and for small `e` in radians, `sin(e) ≈ e`. The notes don't say this.
- `from outside the material:` The `d * e` figure is for a *single* heading error made once. In practice a new heading error comes in at every step, so `e` itself keeps growing as you drive. Position error then grows faster than in proportion to distance. The notes only say it "grows with distance travelled" and don't say how fast.
- `from outside the material:` A worked number: `e = 0.01` rad is about 0.57°, which sounds tiny. After `d = 10` m it gives a miss of about 0.1 m, and after 100 m about 1 m.

`from outside the material:` **Analogy.** Picture walking across a field with a compass that reads half a degree off. If you misjudge one stride's length, you're off by a few centimetres, and that's all. If you're walking on the wrong bearing, every stride takes you a little further from your target, and the miss gets bigger the further you walk. The analogy leaves out one thing: in odometry the bearing error itself keeps growing, as the second gap above explains.

**Check question:** Using the 4.2 update equations, in one sentence: why does an error in `theta` affect every later `x` and `y` update, while an error in a single step's `dS` doesn't?
✗ fail one-question

- `from outside the material:` The `d * e` figure is for a *single* heading error made once. In practice a new heading error comes in at every step, so `e` itself keeps growing as you drive. Position error then grows faster than in proportion to distance. The notes only say it "grows with distance travelled" and don't say how fast.
- `from outside the material:` A worked number: `e = 0.01` rad is about 0.57°, which sounds tiny. After `d = 10` m it gives a miss of about 0.1 m, and after 100 m about 1 m.

`from outside the material:` **Analogy.** Picture walking across a field with a compass that reads half a degree off. If you misjudge one stride's length, you're off by a few centimetres, and that's all. If you're walking on the wrong bearing, every stride takes you a little further from your target, and the miss gets bigger the further you walk. The analogy leaves out one thing: in odometry the bearing error itself keeps growing, as the second gap above explains.

**Check question:** Using the 4.2 update equations, in one sentence: why does an error in `theta` affect every later `x` and `y` update, while an error in a single step's `dS` doesn't?

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
The notes say: *"Odometry error is unbounded: each step adds error that is never removed, so position error grows with distance travelled. Heading error is the worst kind because a heading error of e radians becomes a position error of about d \* e after a further distance d."*

In plain words, there are two claims. First, odometry only ever adds the next small step to its current estimate, and nothing in the method checks that estimate against the world. Any error in one step stays in every later estimate, and new errors keep piling on. Second, of all the errors that can pile up, an error in heading `theta` does the most harm, because it keeps creating new position error for as long as the robot keeps driving.

**Why heading is different, using 4.2.** Look at the pose update in 4.2:

- `x += dS * cos(theta + dtheta/2)`
- `y += dS * sin(theta + dtheta/2)`
- `theta += dtheta`

`theta` is not just one of the outputs. It goes into every later `x` and `y` update, because it sets which way each step of length `dS` is pointed. Compare two kinds of mistake:

1. **A wrong `dS` in one step** (say the wheel travelled slightly further than the encoder counted). The position is off by that small amount, and that error just stays. It doesn't make later steps worse. (My reading: the notes don't make this comparison directly. I'm drawing it from the update equations.)
2. **A wrong `theta`, off by `e`.** Because `theta += dtheta` only adds and never corrects, the error `e` stays in `theta` from then on. Every later step goes into the cos/sin with the wrong angle, so every step points slightly off course. After a further distance `d`, all those slightly-off steps add up to a sideways miss of about `d * e`. That's the 4.3 formula.

So a distance error is a one-off. A heading error keeps growing in proportion to how far you drive afterwards.

**Where heading error comes from (linking to 4.2 and 4.4).** `dtheta = (dS_R - dS_L) / L` depends on the *difference* between the two wheels, divided by the wheelbase `L`. My reading is that this is why the 4.4 sources matter so much. Unequal wheel radii make `dS_R` and `dS_L` slightly wrong relative to each other, and a wrong `L` scales every `dtheta`. Both feed straight into heading, and from heading into position.

**Gaps in the notes.**

- `from outside the material:` The "about" in "about `d * e`" comes from the small-angle approximation. The true sideways miss is `d * sin(e)`, and for small `e` in radians, `sin(e) ≈ e`. The notes don't say this.
- `from outside the material:` The `d * e` figure is for a *single* heading error made once. In practice a new heading error comes in at every step, so `e` itself keeps growing as you drive. Position error then grows faster than in proportion to distance. The notes only say it "grows with distance travelled" and don't say how fast.
- `from outside the material:` A worked number: `e = 0.01` rad is about 0.57°, which sounds tiny. After `d = 10` m it gives a miss of about 0.1 m, and after 100 m about 1 m.

`from outside the material:` **Analogy.** Picture walking across a field with a compass that reads half a degree off. If you misjudge one stride's length, you're off by a few centimetres, and that's all. If you're walking on the wrong bearing, every stride takes you a little further from your target, and the miss gets bigger the further you walk. The analogy leaves out one thing: in odometry the bearing error itself keeps growing, as the second gap above explains.

**Check question:** Using the 4.2 update equations, in one sentence: why does an error in `theta` affect every later `x` and `y` update, while an error in a single step's `dS` doesn't?

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
Section 4.3 says:

> "Odometry error is unbounded: each step adds error that is never removed, so position error grows with distance travelled. Heading error is the worst kind because a heading error of e radians becomes a position error of about d * e after a further distance d."

In plain words: every step adds a small error, nothing in odometry ever corrects it, so the errors pile up the further you drive. A heading error is worse than other errors because it doesn't stay the same size. It keeps turning into more position error with every metre you drive afterwards.

**Why the error is never removed (from 4.2).** Look at the update rules in 4.2:

- `x += dS * cos(theta + dtheta/2)`
- `y += dS * sin(theta + dtheta/2)`
- `theta += dtheta`

Each new pose is the old pose plus an increment. Nothing in these rules compares the robot's estimate with the outside world. So if a step adds error to x, y or theta, the error is carried into every later step. That's what "unbounded" means in 4.3.

**Why heading error is worse than distance error (also 4.2).** Theta is used in two different ways:

1. `theta += dtheta`: an error in dtheta stays in theta from then on.
2. `cos(theta + ...)` and `sin(theta + ...)`: theta sets the direction of every later x and y step.

So a heading error doesn't just sit there. Each later step goes slightly in the wrong direction, and those small sideways errors add up. That's where 4.3 gets "about d * e after a further distance d": drive d further with your heading off by e, and you end up roughly d·e to the side of where you think you are.

Compare an error in dS at one step. It makes that one step too long or too short, and that's it. Later steps still go in the right direction, so that error doesn't get bigger.

**Linking to 4.4.** Two of the systematic sources in 4.4, unequal wheel radii and a wrong wheelbase L, feed straight into `dtheta = (dS_R - dS_L) / L`. That means they produce exactly the kind of error 4.3 says is worst. My reading is that this is why the UMBmark calibration in 4.5 targets those two.

**from outside the material:** Some extra help that isn't in your notes:

- **Where d * e comes from.** If you drive d in a direction that's off by angle e, your sideways miss is d·sin(e). For small angles, sin(e) ≈ e (in radians), so the miss is about d·e. That's why the notes say "about". Your notes don't state this approximation.
- **Example numbers.** A heading error of 0.01 rad (about 0.6°) sounds tiny. After a further 10 m it becomes about 10 × 0.01 = 0.1 m of position error. After 100 m it's about 1 m. A 1 cm error in one dS stays 1 cm.
- **It gets worse than d·e.** The d·e formula assumes one fixed heading error. In a real run, heading error keeps growing step by step (through `theta += dtheta`), so position error can grow faster than in proportion to distance. The notes don't go into this.
- **Analogy.** It's like walking across a field with your compass a degree off. A miscounted step costs you one step. The wrong compass bearing pushes you further off course the longer you walk. Where the analogy breaks down: a person can spot a landmark and correct, but pure odometry has nothing to correct against.

**Check question:** Using the 4.2 update equations, why does an error in dtheta at one step affect x and y at every later step, while an error in dS at one step doesn't? Answer in a sentence and I'll check it against the notes.

# Section 4.3: why heading error is the worst

The section makes two points. The second explains the first.

## Point 1: odometry error is unbounded

From 4.2, each pose update adds a small change to the old pose:

```
x += dS * cos(theta + dtheta/2)
theta += dtheta
```

Nothing in these equations checks the pose against the outside world. If `dS` or `dtheta` is slightly wrong in one step, that mistake becomes part of `x`, `y` and `theta`, and every later step builds on it. Errors pile up and never shrink. That's why the error grows with distance travelled: more steps means more small mistakes added together.

## Point 2: heading error gets multiplied by future distance

There are two kinds of mistake a step can make:

- **Distance error** (wrong `dS`): the robot thinks it moved 1.000 m but actually moved 1.002 m. That leaves the position estimate 2 mm off, and it stays 2 mm off. Later steps don't make it bigger.
- **Heading error** (wrong `theta`): the robot thinks it's facing 0 rad but is really facing `e` rad. From then on, every metre it drives goes in a slightly wrong direction. The update uses `cos(theta)` and `sin(theta)` with the wrong `theta`, so each step's position change points slightly off. Over a further distance `d`, the true path and the estimated path spread apart like two lines from the same point at angle `e`:

```
            true path
           /
          /  ← gap ≈ d · e
         /
start ●──────────────── estimated path
         <----- d ----->
```

The exact gap is `d · sin(e)`. For small angles `sin(e) ≈ e` (in radians), which gives the notes' "about `d * e`".

So a distance error is a one-off cost. A heading error keeps costing you on every metre you drive afterwards, and the further you go, the larger the position error it causes.

## Why heading errors are also easy to get

Look at 4.2 again: `dtheta = (dS_R - dS_L) / L`. Heading depends on the difference between the two wheel distances, divided by the wheelbase. `L` is usually small (tens of cm), so a tiny wheel mismatch becomes a noticeable angle.

**Worked example.** Take `L = 0.3 m`, and suppose the right wheel's distance is wrong by 1 mm in one step:

- Error in `dS` = 1 mm / 2 = **0.5 mm**, and it stays 0.5 mm.
- Error in `dtheta` = 0.001 / 0.3 ≈ **0.0033 rad** (about 0.2°).
- After driving another 10 m, that heading error gives about 10 × 0.0033 ≈ **3.3 cm** of position error.

The same 1 mm slip causes a position error roughly 70 times larger through heading than through distance, and the gap keeps widening the further the robot drives.

## How this links to 4.4 and 4.5

Two of the systematic errors in 4.4 hit `dtheta` directly:

- Unequal wheel radii make `dS_R − dS_L` wrong on every step, so the robot drifts into a curve.
- A wrong `L` scales every `dtheta` incorrectly, so every turn is over- or under-estimated.

Because heading error is so damaging, these two are the ones worth calibrating. That is exactly what UMBmark in 4.5 is designed to separate and measure.

**One-line summary:** a distance error stays the size it was, but a heading error gets multiplied by every metre you drive afterwards, and `dtheta`'s division by a small `L` makes heading errors easy to pick up.

# Section 4.3: why heading error is the worst kind

## Part 1: "Odometry error is unbounded"

Look at the pose update in 4.2:

```
x += dS * cos(theta + dtheta/2)
y += dS * sin(theta + dtheta/2)
theta += dtheta
```

Each new pose is the old pose plus a small increment. If an increment is slightly wrong (from slip, a wrong wheel radius, and so on), the error goes into x, y or theta. Nothing in these equations ever corrects it. The robot only measures its own wheels and has no outside reference, such as a landmark or GPS, to check against. So errors keep adding up, and the more steps (distance) the robot takes, the more error it carries. That is what "unbounded" means.

## Part 2: Why heading error is worse than position error

Compare two mistakes the robot could make at some moment.

**A position error.** Say the robot thinks it is 5 cm to the left of where it really is. If it then drives 10 m perfectly, it is still 5 cm off. The error stays the same size as it drives on.

**A heading error.** Say the robot thinks it is pointing 1° further left than it really is. Every later step uses that wrong theta in `cos(theta)` and `sin(theta)`. So each step's movement is added in a slightly wrong direction, and the estimated path gradually swings away from the real one:

```
            estimated path
           /
          /  ← gap grows with distance
         /
start ●───────────────── true path
      ↑ angle e
```

The two paths split at angle e. After a further distance d, the gap between them is an arc of radius d and angle e, which has length **d · e**. That is the formula in your notes. (Strictly, the gap is the chord 2d·sin(e/2), but for small angles that is about d·e.)

**Worked example:** 1° = 0.0175 rad. After 10 m, the gap is 10 × 0.0175 ≈ **17.5 cm**. After 100 m it is about **1.75 m**. One small angle mistake keeps producing more position error the further the robot goes.

So the difference is:
- A position error is a fixed offset.
- A heading error is an offset that keeps growing with distance.

## It compounds: a step beyond your notes

The heading errors also add up step after step. If the heading error itself grows with distance, and each bit of heading error then gets multiplied by the remaining distance, the position error grows faster than linearly. For a steady bias, it grows roughly with d². Your notes don't say this, but it's the usual reason given for why heading is "the worst".

## How this connects to 4.2 and 4.4

Heading errors are also easy to cause. From 4.2, `dtheta = (dS_R - dS_L) / L` depends on the *difference* between the two wheel distances. A tiny mismatch, such as one wheel radius being 0.5% larger (an "unequal wheel radii" error from 4.4), makes the robot think it turned when it actually went straight. That small fake turn then grows through d · e. A wrong wheelbase L scales every dtheta, so it gives you a heading error on every turn.

This is why UMBmark (4.5) focuses on those two parameters: they are the systematic sources that cause heading error.

# Section 4.3: why heading error is the worst kind

## Part 1: "Odometry error is unbounded"

Look at the pose update in 4.2:

```
x += dS * cos(theta + dtheta/2)
y += dS * sin(theta + dtheta/2)
theta += dtheta
```

Each new pose is the old pose plus a small increment. If an increment is slightly wrong (from slip, a wrong wheel radius, and so on), the error goes into x, y or theta. Nothing in these equations ever corrects it. The robot only measures its own wheels and has no outside reference, such as a landmark or GPS, to check against. So errors keep adding up, and the more steps (distance) the robot takes, the more error it carries. That is what "unbounded" means.

## Part 2: Why heading error is worse than position error

Compare two mistakes the robot could make at some moment.

**A position error.** Say the robot thinks it is 5 cm to the left of where it really is. If it then drives 10 m perfectly, it is still 5 cm off. The error stays the same size as it drives on.

**A heading error.** Say the robot thinks it is pointing 1° further left than it really is. Every later step uses that wrong theta in `cos(theta)` and `sin(theta)`. So each step's movement is added in a slightly wrong direction, and the estimated path gradually swings away from the real one:

```
            estimated path
           /
          /  ← gap grows with distance
         /
start ●───────────────── true path
      ↑ angle e
```

The two paths split at angle e. After a further distance d, the gap between them is an arc of radius d and angle e, which has length **d · e**. That is the formula in your notes. (Strictly, the gap is the chord 2d·sin(e/2), but for small angles that is about d·e.)

**Worked example:** 1° = 0.0175 rad. After 10 m, the gap is 10 × 0.0175 ≈ **17.5 cm**. After 100 m it is about **1.75 m**. One small angle mistake keeps producing more position error the further the robot goes.

So the difference is:
- A position error is a fixed offset.
- A heading error is an offset that keeps growing with distance.

## It compounds: a step beyond your notes

The heading errors also add up step after step. If the heading error itself grows with distance, and each bit of heading error then gets multiplied by the remaining distance, the position error grows faster than linearly. For a steady bias, it grows roughly with d². Your notes don't say this, but it's the usual reason given for why heading is "the worst".

## How this connects to 4.2 and 4.4

Heading errors are also easy to cause. From 4.2, `dtheta = (dS_R - dS_L) / L` depends on the *difference* between the two wheel distances. A tiny mismatch, such as one wheel radius being 0.5% larger (an "unequal wheel radii" error from 4.4), makes the robot think it turned when it actually went straight. That small fake turn then grows through d · e. A wrong wheelbase L scales every dtheta, so it gives you a heading error on every turn.

This is why UMBmark (4.5) focuses on those two parameters: they are the systematic sources that cause heading error.

# Section 4.3: why heading error is the worst

## Part 1: "Odometry error is unbounded"

Odometry has no outside reference. The robot never sees a landmark or GPS fix that tells it where it really is. It only adds small increments (4.2) to its last estimate:

```
x += dS * cos(theta + dtheta/2)
```

If one step's `dS` is slightly wrong, that error is now part of `x`, and no later step removes it. The next step adds its own small error on top. Errors keep piling up, so the longer the robot drives, the larger the position error gets. That is what "unbounded" means here.

## Part 2: Why heading error is the worst

Compare the two kinds of mistake.

**Position error stays the same size.** Suppose one step overestimates distance by 1 cm. The robot thinks it is 1 cm further along than it is. Every later step is still computed correctly relative to that, so the error stays at 1 cm. It doesn't grow.

**Heading error grows with every step.** Suppose `theta` is wrong by a small angle `e`. Look at the update again: `theta` sits inside the `cos` and `sin` of **every future step**. So every metre the robot drives after that is added in a slightly wrong direction. The real path and the estimated path spread apart like two lines from the same point at an angle `e`:

```
            estimated path
          /
         /   ← gap ≈ d · e
        /
start  ●——————————————— true path
        <——— d ———>
```

After a further distance `d`, the gap is `d · sin(e)`. For small angles `sin(e) ≈ e`, which gives the notes' **d · e**. It's the same arc-length idea as `s = r·θ`.

**Example:** a 1° heading error (≈ 0.0175 rad):

| Distance driven after the error | Position error |
|---|---|
| 1 m | ~1.7 cm |
| 10 m | ~17 cm |
| 100 m | ~1.7 m |

A single 1 cm distance error stays at 1 cm. A single 1° heading error keeps growing for as long as the robot moves.

## Part 3: Why heading errors are easy to get

The notes don't say this directly, but it follows from 4.2:

```
dtheta = (dS_R - dS_L) / L
```

Heading depends on the **difference** between the two wheels, **divided by the wheelbase L**, which is usually small (say 0.3 m). So a tiny mismatch turns into a noticeable angle. If the right wheel's distance is overestimated by just 1 mm in one step, that gives 0.001 / 0.3 ≈ 0.0033 rad (≈ 0.2°) of heading error. By Part 2, that error then grows with every metre driven.

This connects to 4.4. Unequal wheel radii make `dS_R − dS_L` slightly wrong on every step. A wrong `L` scales every `dtheta` wrongly. Both are systematic heading errors. Because they happen on every step, the heading error itself keeps growing, and position error grows even faster than the d · e from a single error.

This is also why UMBmark (4.5) exists: it is a way to measure and correct exactly these two heading-error sources.

## One-line summary

A position error is a one-time offset. A heading error points every later step in the wrong direction, so its effect keeps growing (≈ d · e), and small wheel mismatches become noticeable heading errors because of the division by L.

Prompt

I don't get section 4.3, especially why heading error is the worst. Explain it from my notes.

Lecture 4: Wheel odometry (notes)

4.1 Differential drive. A differential-drive robot has two driven wheels of radius r on an axle of length L (the wheelbase). If the left and right wheels turn by angles dphi_L and dphi_R in a time step, the wheels travel dS_L = r * dphi_L and dS_R = r * dphi_R along the ground.

4.2 Pose update. The distance travelled by the robot's centre is dS = (dS_R + dS_L) / 2 and the change of heading is dtheta = (dS_R - dS_L) / L. With heading theta, the pose update is x += dS * cos(theta + dtheta/2), y += dS * sin(theta + dtheta/2), theta += dtheta. The half-angle term is used because the robot turns during the step, so the mean heading over the step is a better estimate than the heading at its start.

4.3 Error growth. Odometry error is unbounded: each step adds error that is never removed, so position error grows with distance travelled. Heading error is the worst kind because a heading error of e radians becomes a position error of about d * e after a further distance d.

4.4 Sources of error. The notes list three systematic sources: unequal wheel radii, an inaccurate wheelbase L, and finite encoder resolution. Wheel slip is a non-systematic source; it cannot be calibrated out.

4.5 Calibration. The UMBmark method drives the robot around a square path clockwise and anticlockwise and uses the two end-position errors to separate the effect of unequal wheel radii from the effect of a wrong wheelbase.

- *"The error can be fixed later by the Kalman filter."* Your notes don't mention a Kalman filter, so I can't confirm this. §4.3 also says odometry error is "unbounded: each step adds error that is never removed". Within these notes, that sentence points the other way from your claim.

**Next step**
Reread the last sentence of §4.3. The question asks *why* heading error is worse, and the notes answer with a mechanism: a small angle error keeps getting multiplied by every further distance travelled (≈ d·e). The answer needs to name that mechanism, not a cause of the error like slip.

**Model answer (from your notes)**
The pose update uses θ + Δθ/2 because the robot turns during the step. The mean heading over the step is therefore a better estimate of the direction it travelled than the heading at the start (§4.2). Heading error is the worst kind of odometry error because it turns into position error that keeps growing. A heading error of e radians becomes a position error of about d·e after a further distance d (§4.3).

Your first part matches your notes. The second part gives a reason your notes don't give, and the Kalman filter point conflicts with them.

**1. Why theta + dtheta/2: correct.**
Your notes (4.2) say the robot turns during the step, so the mean heading over the step is a better estimate than the heading at its start. That's what you said. To match the notes more closely, say "a better estimate than the heading at the start of the step," so it's clear what the half angle improves on.

**2. Why heading error is worse: wrong reason.**
- Your notes (4.3) never say wheels slip more when turning. Their reason is that heading error grows into position error. A heading error of *e* radians turns into a position error of about **d·e** after the robot travels a further distance *d*. A small angle error keeps adding position error with every step after it. That is the key idea, and your answer leaves it out.
- Slip does appear in your notes (4.4), but only as a *non-systematic* source of error in general. Nothing links it to turning or to heading error in particular.

**3. "Can be fixed later by the Kalman filter": remove it.**
- Your notes (4.3) say the opposite: odometry error is *unbounded*, and each step adds error that is *never removed*.
- Kalman filters aren't in these notes. A filter that combines odometry with other sensors can correct drift, but that's beyond this lecture.
- It also doesn't answer the question, which asks why heading error is worse, not how to fix it.

**A suggested answer based on your notes:**
> The robot turns during the step, so the mean heading over the step (theta + dtheta/2) is a better estimate of its direction of travel than the heading at the start. Heading error is the worst kind because odometry error is never removed, and a heading error of e radians turns into a position error of about d·e after a further distance d, so it keeps adding position error as the robot moves.

Judge votes✗ ✗ ✗
Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
**Part 1 (half angle): Correct.** This matches 4.2. The robot turns during the step, so the heading halfway through the step (theta + dtheta/2) estimates the direction of travel better than the heading at the start. To be more precise, you could say it's the *mean* heading over the step, not just the heading "in the middle".

**Part 2 (why heading error is worse): Wrong reason.** Your notes don't say slip is worse when turning. 4.3 gives a different reason: heading error gets **amplified into position error**. If the heading is off by e radians, then after driving a further distance d the position is off by about **d × e**. So a small heading error keeps causing new position error for every metre the robot travels. A position error doesn't grow like that on its own. This is the key idea the question is testing.

Slip is in your notes, but in 4.4, as a non-systematic error source that can't be calibrated out. That's a separate point, and the notes never link it to turning.

**Part 3 (Kalman filter): Not supported, and it goes against the point of 4.3.** The notes say odometry error is unbounded: each step adds error that is **never removed**. Kalman filters aren't in these notes. If you mean that combining odometry with other sensors can limit the error, that's true in general, but it's not part of this answer. Saying "it can be fixed later" also weakens the main message that odometry alone builds up error without limit. I'd remove it.

**Suggested rewrite:**
> The robot turns during the step, so the mean heading over the step, theta + dtheta/2, is a better estimate of its direction of travel than the heading at the start. Heading error is the worst kind of error because it gets amplified: a heading error of e radians produces a position error of about d·e after a further distance d. Odometry error is unbounded, so this keeps growing as the robot travels.

Prompt

Check my answer against my notes.

Question: Why does the pose update use theta + dtheta/2 rather than theta, and why is heading error worse than position error?

My answer: The half angle is used because the robot is turning during the step, so you take the heading in the middle of the step. Heading error is worse because the wheels slip more when turning. Also, the error can be fixed later by the Kalman filter.

Notes:

Lecture 4: Wheel odometry (notes)

4.1 Differential drive. A differential-drive robot has two driven wheels of radius r on an axle of length L (the wheelbase). If the left and right wheels turn by angles dphi_L and dphi_R in a time step, the wheels travel dS_L = r * dphi_L and dS_R = r * dphi_R along the ground.

4.2 Pose update. The distance travelled by the robot's centre is dS = (dS_R + dS_L) / 2 and the change of heading is dtheta = (dS_R - dS_L) / L. With heading theta, the pose update is x += dS * cos(theta + dtheta/2), y += dS * sin(theta + dtheta/2), theta += dtheta. The half-angle term is used because the robot turns during the step, so the mean heading over the step is a better estimate than the heading at its start.

4.3 Error growth. Odometry error is unbounded: each step adds error that is never removed, so position error grows with distance travelled. Heading error is the worst kind because a heading error of e radians becomes a position error of about d * e after a further distance d.

4.4 Sources of error. The notes list three systematic sources: unequal wheel radii, an inaccurate wheelbase L, and finite encoder resolution. Wheel slip is a non-systematic source; it cannot be calibrated out.

4.5 Calibration. The UMBmark method drives the robot around a square path clockwise and anticlockwise and uses the two end-position errors to separate the effect of unequal wheel radii from the effect of a wrong wheelbase.

next-stepllm

This grader checks one thing: the answer ends with exactly one concrete next step tied to the material (a passage to reread, named by section or quote, or one habit to change). The judge sees only the answer. PASS if there is one such step; FAIL if there is none or if a list of several next steps is given instead.

score-formllm

This grader checks one thing: how the score is given. The judge sees only the answer.

PASS only if the score is given as a fraction of points (for example 1/3, 2/4) and NO percentage, letter grade or predicted exam mark ("would get", "would score", "B+", "70%") appears anywhere. FAIL otherwise.

verdictsllmweight ×2

This grader checks one thing: the grade matches the material. The judge sees only the answer. For reference: the user's answer made three claims. (1) The half angle is used because the robot turns during the step, so the mid-step heading is used: this is RIGHT (section 4.2 says exactly this). (2) Heading error is worse because wheels slip more when turning: this is WRONG; the material says heading error is worse because a heading error of e radians becomes a position error of about d*e after a further distance d, and says nothing about slip when turning. (3) The error can be fixed later by a Kalman filter: the material says nothing about Kalman filters; it says odometry error is never removed.

PASS only if ALL hold: claim (1) is marked right; claim (2) is marked wrong (or not supported) AND the d*e reason from the material is given as what the notes actually say; claim (3) is not marked right (it may be marked wrong, unsupported, or "cannot confirm from your material"). FAIL otherwise.

After that, the rest goes in your notes' order: 4.2, 4.3, Lecture 5, Lecture 6.

# Revision plan: mobile robotics exam, Monday 2026-10-19

**7 days until the exam (6 study days, Sunday off) · 14 h available · the plan needs about 12 h 40 min, leaving about 1 h 20 min of slack.**

I used 15 minutes per page for a first pass, which makes 27 pages about 6 h 45 min. That rate is my assumption. If your notes take you longer, see "What does not fit" below. Repeats take about a third of the first-pass time.

**Order and why:** 4.5 Calibration comes first and most often (5 sessions) because it's your weak spot. On day 1 I pair it with 4.1 Differential drive and 4.4 Sources of error. My reading is that 4.5 builds on them, since it follows "Sources of error" in your notes. I can't confirm that from the contents list alone. After that, the rest goes in your notes' order: 4.2, 4.3, Lecture 5, Lecture 6.

| Date | Hours | First pass | Repeats | How |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Mon 2026-10-12 | 2 h | 4.1 Differential drive (30m), 4.4 Sources of error (30m), **4.5 Calibration (60m)** | none | Read, then redo any 4.5 derivation by hand without the notes |
| Tue 2026-10-13 | 2 h | 4.2 Pose update (45m), 4.3 Error growth (30m), L5 Range sensors pp. 1–2 (30m) | **4.5 (15m)** | Cards on 4.5; work through the 4.2 pose-update equations by hand |
| Wed 2026-10-14 | 2 h | L5 Range sensors pp. 3–6 (60m) | 4.1 (10m), 4.4 (10m), **4.5 (25m)** | Quiz on 4.1, 4.4 and 4.5; **15m slack** |
| Thu 2026-10-15 | 2 h | L6 Probabilistic localisation pp. 1–4 (60m) | 4.2 (15m), 4.3 (10m) | Quiz on 4.2 and 4.3; **35m slack** for anything that slipped |
| Fri 2026-10-16 | 2 h | L6 pp. 5–8 (60m) | L5 (30m), **4.5 (20m)** | Cards on L5; 4.5 by hand; **10m slack** |
| Sat 2026-10-17 | 4 h | **nothing new** | L6 (40m), L5 (30m), 4.1–4.4 together (45m), **4.5 (45m)**, mixed quiz across everything (60m) | Quiz, or a short mock exam if you have a past paper; **20m slack** |
| Sun 2026-10-18 | 0 h | none | none | Day off, as you asked |
| **Mon 2026-10-19** | | **Exam** | | |

## What does not fit
- **At 15 min/page, everything fits.** One weakness: Lecture 6 is the biggest section (8 pages) and gets only one repeat, on Saturday, one day after you finish it. A week leaves no room for more spacing.
- **If you need about 20 min/page,** the first pass grows to about 9 h and the plan no longer fits in 14 h. You'd have to choose:
  - (a) drop Saturday's 60m mixed quiz and use the slack slots; or
  - (b) skim part of Lecture 5 or 6 and say which pages are lowest priority for you.
  
  Please don't cut the 4.5 sessions.

# Revision plan: Mon 12 to Mon 19 Oct

**Time available:** 5 weekdays × 2h + Saturday 4h = **14 hours**. Sunday is off, and I haven't counted exam day.

**Approach:** Lecture 4 comes first, in order, because calibration (4.5) corrects the errors described in 4.3 and 4.4. Calibration gets a full session of its own. You then come back to it twice more, spaced out and from memory. Lecture 6 is the largest topic, so it gets two sessions.

| Day | Hours | What to do |
|---|---|---|
| **Mon 12** | 2h | **4.1 Differential drive + 4.2 Pose update** (5 pp). Derive the kinematic equations yourself. Then work through one pose update by hand, from wheel encoder ticks to Δs and Δθ to the new (x, y, θ). |
| **Tue 13** | 2h | **4.3 Error growth + 4.4 Sources of error** (4 pp). Make sure you can tell **systematic** errors (unequal wheel diameters, wrong wheelbase) from **non-systematic** ones (slip, bumps). Calibration only fixes systematic errors. Spend the last 15 min sketching how the error ellipse grows. |
| **Wed 14** | 2h | **4.5 Calibration, first pass** (4 pp, the whole session). Go slowly. Know the procedure step by step: the test path, what you measure, and how the correction factors come from the measurements. Write a one-page summary sheet. |
| **Thu 15** | 2h | **Lecture 5: Range sensors** (6 pp, 1.5h). Cover sensor types, their error models and their failure modes. **Then 30 min of calibration recall:** rebuild your summary sheet from memory and check it against the notes. |
| **Fri 16** | 2h | **Lecture 6: Probabilistic localisation, first half** (about pp 1–4). Bayes filter, motion model and sensor model. Link the motion model back to 4.3 and 4.4 on error growth. |
| **Sat 17** | 4h | **1.5h:** Lecture 6 second half (about pp 5–8). **1h: calibration second pass.** Do worked problems or past-paper questions on 4.5 without notes. **1.5h:** a mixed practice paper or questions across all three lectures, under timed conditions if you can. |
| **Sun 18** | 0h | Rest. |
| **Mon 19** | — | **Exam.** If you have time beforehand, a 15-minute look over your calibration and formula summary sheets is enough. Don't learn anything new. |

**Calibration total:** about 3.5h for 4 pages, in three spaced sessions (Wed, Thu, Sat). That's roughly twice the time per page of any other topic.

**If you fall behind:** cut the mixed practice on Saturday first. Keep the calibration second pass.

Here's a plan using the 14 hours you have: five weekdays at 2h each, plus 4h on Saturday. Sunday is off. I've treated Monday the 19th as exam day, not a study day.

**How it's split:** Lecture 4 comes first because calibration (4.5) depends on error growth (4.3) and sources of error (4.4). Calibration gets three separate sessions spread over the week, which helps it stick better than one long session.

| Day | Hours | Topic | What to do |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Mon 12** | 2h | 4.1 Differential drive (2pp) + 4.2 Pose update (3pp) | Derive the kinematics and pose-update equations yourself, then work one numerical example. |
| **Tue 13** | 2h | 4.3 Error growth (2pp) + 4.4 Sources of error (2pp) | Be clear on systematic vs. non-systematic errors and why error grows with distance. **Last 15 min:** skim 4.5 as a preview. |
| **Wed 14** | 2h | **4.5 Calibration (4pp), first pass** | Read slowly. Rework every derivation and worked example by hand. Write a one-page summary of the procedure and the formulas. |
| **Thu 15** | 30m | **Calibration recall** | Close the notes and rewrite your summary from memory. Check it and fix the gaps. |
| | 1.5h | Lecture 5: Range sensors (6pp) | Sensor types, how they work, error characteristics |
| **Fri 16** | 2h | Lecture 6: Probabilistic localisation, pp. 1–4 | Core ideas and the main algorithm steps |
| **Sat 17** | 1.25h | Lecture 6, pp. 5–8 | Finish the lecture. Trace one full localisation update by hand. |
| | 1h | **Calibration, second pass** | Do problems or past-paper questions without notes. Only check the notes afterwards. |
| | 1.75h | Mixed review | Past paper or self-quiz across all three lectures. Spend the most time on whatever went worst. |
| **Sun 18** | 0 | Rest | |
| **Mon 19** | | **Exam** | If you want, glance at your calibration summary sheet beforehand. |

**Totals:** Lecture 4 gets 7.75h, of which 3.5h is calibration. Lecture 5 gets 1.5h, Lecture 6 gets 3.25h, and mixed review gets 1.75h.

**If you fall behind:** cut the Saturday mixed review first. Don't skip the calibration recall on Thursday or the calibration practice on Saturday.

# Revision plan: Mon 12 Oct to exam on Mon 19 Oct

**Time available:** 14 hours (5 weekdays × 2h, plus 4h on Saturday). **Material:** 27 pages. Calibration (4.5) gets about 4h in total: one deep session, short recall sessions afterwards, and practice problems on Saturday. 4.3 and 4.4 come right before it because calibration corrects the systematic errors those sections introduce.

| Day | Hours | Focus | What to do |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Mon 12** | 2h | 4.1 Differential drive (2p), 4.2 Pose update (3p) | Derive the kinematics yourself: wheel speeds → v, ω → pose update. Work one numeric example by hand. |
| **Tue 13** | 2h | 4.3 Error growth (2p), 4.4 Sources of error (2p) | Sort the error sources into **systematic** (e.g. unequal wheel diameters, uncertain wheelbase) and **non-systematic** (e.g. slip, uneven floor). Know why error grows without bound. This sets up calibration. |
| **Wed 14** | 2h | **4.5 Calibration (4p): deep pass** | Read slowly and re-derive every equation. Note which errors calibration corrects and how, and the test procedure (e.g. a square path in both directions, if your notes use UMBmark). End with a one-page summary written from memory. |
| **Thu 15** | 2h | 15 min calibration recall, then Lecture 5 Range sensors (6p) | Recall: rewrite the calibration summary without notes and check it. Then for each sensor type cover how it works, its error characteristics and its failure modes. |
| **Fri 16** | 2h | 15 min calibration recall, then Lecture 6 Probabilistic localisation (8p) | This is the biggest block, so aim to understand it, not memorise it. Cover belief, the motion model and the sensor model, and the predict/update cycle. Link the motion model back to Lecture 4's error growth. |
| **Sat 17** | 4h | Consolidation and practice | **1h:** calibration problems (second pass, fix anything shaky). **1h:** Lecture 6 again, plus a worked localisation example. **1.5h:** a timed mixed practice set or past paper. **30 min:** go over mistakes and make a final one-page summary sheet. |
| **Sun 18** | none | Rest | |
| **Mon 19** | about 15 min | Exam day | Optional quick look at the summary sheet. Don't learn anything new. |

**Tips:**
- **Buffer:** If a weekday runs over, take the time from Saturday's practice block. Don't cut the calibration recall sessions.
- **Recall method:** Start each session with 5 minutes recalling the previous day's topic before opening the notes.
- **If calibration still feels weak on Thursday:** Swap Saturday's Lecture 6 hour for more calibration problems. Lecture 6 builds on ideas you'll already have, so it's easier to recover.

Prompt

Today is Monday 2026-10-12. My mobile robotics exam is on Monday 2026-10-19. I can do 2 hours a day on weekdays and 4 hours on Saturday, nothing on Sunday. My weak spot is calibration (4.5). Plan my revision from these notes.

Table of contents of the notes (sizes in pages): 4.1 Differential drive (2), 4.2 Pose update (3), 4.3 Error growth (2), 4.4 Sources of error (2), 4.5 Calibration (4). Lecture 5: Range sensors (6). Lecture 6: Probabilistic localisation (8).

Graders — what "good" means for this case
datesllmweight ×2

This grader checks one thing: the plan's dates. The judge sees only the answer. For reference: today is Monday 2026-10-12 and the exam is Monday 2026-10-19, so the plan covers 2026-10-12 to 2026-10-18 (seven days: Mon 12, Tue 13, Wed 14, Thu 15, Fri 16, Sat 17, Sun 18). Sunday has no hours.

PASS only if the plan's table runs over those dates with the correct weekdays, gives Sunday 2026-10-18 no study hours (or marks it free), and the last working day before the exam (Saturday 17 or, if Sunday is used for nothing, the final planned day) contains repeats only and nothing new. FAIL on a wrong weekday, a date outside the range, or new material on the final planned day.

fitllmweight ×2

This grader checks one thing: the hours add up and the weak spot comes first. The judge sees only the answer. For reference: the user has 2 hours on each of Mon 12, Tue 13, Wed 14, Thu 15, Fri 16 and 4 hours on Sat 17: 14 hours in all. The weak spot is section 4.5 Calibration.

no-promisellm

This grader checks one thing: no predicted outcome. The judge sees only the answer. FAIL if the plan says or implies what grade or result the user will get ("you'll be ready", "you'll pass", "this guarantees"). A plain statement that the plan covers all the material is fine. Otherwise PASS.

**Question 1 of 8**

A differential-drive robot's left and right wheels travel distances dS_L and dS_R in one time step. The wheelbase is L.

Write the formulas for:
- **(a)** dS, the distance travelled by the robot's centre
- **(b)** dθ, the change in heading

Prompt

Quiz me on these notes. 8 questions.

Lecture 4: Wheel odometry (notes)

4.1 Differential drive. A differential-drive robot has two driven wheels of radius r on an axle of length L (the wheelbase). If the left and right wheels turn by angles dphi_L and dphi_R in a time step, the wheels travel dS_L = r * dphi_L and dS_R = r * dphi_R along the ground.

4.2 Pose update. The distance travelled by the robot's centre is dS = (dS_R + dS_L) / 2 and the change of heading is dtheta = (dS_R - dS_L) / L. With heading theta, the pose update is x += dS * cos(theta + dtheta/2), y += dS * sin(theta + dtheta/2), theta += dtheta. The half-angle term is used because the robot turns during the step, so the mean heading over the step is a better estimate than the heading at its start.

4.3 Error growth. Odometry error is unbounded: each step adds error that is never removed, so position error grows with distance travelled. Heading error is the worst kind because a heading error of e radians becomes a position error of about d * e after a further distance d.

4.4 Sources of error. The notes list three systematic sources: unequal wheel radii, an inaccurate wheelbase L, and finite encoder resolution. Wheel slip is a non-systematic source; it cannot be calibrated out.

4.5 Calibration. The UMBmark method drives the robot around a square path clockwise and anticlockwise and uses the two end-position errors to separate the effect of unequal wheel radii from the effect of a wrong wheelbase.
Graders — what "good" means for this case
groundedllmweight ×2

This grader checks one thing: every question can be answered from the material. The judge sees only the answer.

For each question, decide whether the material below contains its answer. A question that applies the material's rule to a new small situation is fine if the situation is marked "from outside the material" and the rule is in the material. FAIL if any question needs a fact the material does not state (what an encoder is, what UMBmark stands for, anything about gyroscopes, IMUs, GPS, Kalman filters, lidar, cameras, SLAM, units, or numeric values for r or L) and is not marked as from outside the material. Otherwise PASS.

For reference, the material the user gave is below. Everything the user knows is in it. In particular the material does NOT say: what an encoder is or how it works, what UMBmark stands for, any numeric value for r or L, anything about gyroscopes, IMUs, GPS, Kalman filters, lidar, cameras, visual odometry, or SLAM, and it does not give the units of any quantity.

<material> Lecture 4: Wheel odometry (notes)

4.1 Differential drive. A differential-drive robot has two driven wheels of radius r on an axle of length L (the wheelbase). If the left and right wheels turn by angles dphi_L and dphi_R in a time step, the wheels travel dS_L = r * dphi_L and dS_R = r * dphi_R along the ground.

4.2 Pose update. The distance travelled by the robot's centre is dS = (dS_R + dS_L) / 2 and the change of heading is dtheta = (dS_R - dS_L) / L. With heading theta, the pose update is x += dS * cos(theta + dtheta/2), y += dS * sin(theta + dtheta/2), theta += dtheta. The half-angle term is used because the robot turns during the step, so the mean heading over the step is a better estimate than the heading at its start.

4.3 Error growth. Odometry error is unbounded: each step adds error that is never removed, so position error grows with distance travelled. Heading error is the worst kind because a heading error of e radians becomes a position error of about d * e after a further distance d.

4.4 Sources of error. The notes list three systematic sources: unequal wheel radii, an inaccurate wheelbase L, and finite encoder resolution. Wheel slip is a non-systematic source; it cannot be calibrated out.

4.5 Calibration. The UMBmark method drives the robot around a square path clockwise and anticlockwise and uses the two end-position errors to separate the effect of unequal wheel radii from the effect of a wrong wheelbase. </material>

hidden-keyllmweight ×2

This grader checks one thing: the answers are withheld. The judge sees only the assistant's reply to a request for a quiz.

PASS only if the reply gives numbered questions and does NOT give their answers, model answers or an answer key in the same reply (a sentence saying the key comes after the user answers is expected). FAIL if any question is followed by its answer, or an answer key appears anywhere in the reply.

kindsllm

# اختبار: المحاضرة 4 — قياس المسافة بالعجلات

أجب عن أي عدد من الأسئلة. سأصحّح إجاباتك بناءً على الملخص فقط، وأعرض مفتاح الإجابات في النهاية.

---

**1. (استرجاع)**
اكتب الصيغتين اللتين يذكرهما الملخص لـ:
- المسافة التي يقطعها مركز الروبوت `dS`
- تغيّر الاتجاه `dtheta`

بدلالة `dS_L` و `dS_R` و `L`.

**2. (استرجاع)**
يذكر الملخص ثلاثة مصادر **منهجية** للخطأ ومصدرًا واحدًا **غير منهجي**. اذكرها كلها، وما الذي يقوله الملخص عن المصدر غير المنهجي؟

**3. (فهم)**
لماذا يُستخدم `theta + dtheta/2` عند تحديث الوضعية بدلًا من `theta` في بداية الخطوة؟

**4. (فهم)**
يقول الملخص إن خطأ الاتجاه هو "الأسوأ". اشرح السبب كما يعرضه الملخص، واشرح أيضًا لماذا يُوصف خطأ قياس المسافة بأنه "غير محدود".

**5. (تطبيق)**
`من خارج المادة:` لروبوت ذي دفع تفاضلي المعطيات التالية:
- نصف قطر كل عجلة `r = 0.1 m`
- قاعدة العجلات `L = 0.5 m`

في خطوة زمنية واحدة دارت العجلة اليسرى بزاوية `dphi_L = 2 rad`، ودارت اليمنى بزاوية `dphi_R = 4 rad`.

احسب `dS_L` و `dS_R` و `dS` و `dtheta`.

**6. (تطبيق)**
`من خارج المادة:` لدى روبوت خطأ اتجاه مقداره `e = 0.02 rad`، ثم قطع بعد ذلك مسافة إضافية `d = 50 m`.

ما خطأ الموضع التقريبي الناتج عن خطأ الاتجاه هذا وحده، حسب القاعدة الواردة في الملخص؟

**1. (استرجاع)**
اكتب الصيغتين اللتين يذكرهما الملخص لـ:
- المسافة التي يقطعها مركز الروبوت `dS`
- تغيّر الاتجاه `dtheta`

بدلالة `dS_L` و `dS_R` و `L`.

**2. (استرجاع)**
يذكر الملخص ثلاثة مصادر **منهجية** للخطأ ومصدرًا واحدًا **غير منهجي**. اذكرها كلها، وما الذي يقوله الملخص عن المصدر غير المنهجي؟

**3. (فهم)**
لماذا يُستخدم `theta + dtheta/2` عند تحديث الوضعية بدلًا من `theta` في بداية الخطوة؟

**4. (فهم)**
يقول الملخص إن خطأ الاتجاه هو "الأسوأ". اشرح السبب كما يعرضه الملخص، واشرح أيضًا لماذا يُوصف خطأ قياس المسافة بأنه "غير محدود".

**5. (تطبيق)**
`من خارج المادة:` لروبوت ذي دفع تفاضلي المعطيات التالية:
- نصف قطر كل عجلة `r = 0.1 m`
- قاعدة العجلات `L = 0.5 m`

احسب `dS_L` و `dS_R` و `dS` و `dtheta`.

**2. [استرجاع]**
ما مصادر الخطأ المنهجية الثلاثة في قياس المسافة بالعجلات؟ وما المصدر غير المنهجي الذي يذكره الملخص؟ وما الذي يميّزه عن المصادر المنهجية؟

# اختبار: المحاضرة 4، قياس المسافة بالعجلات

أجب عن الأسئلة التي تريدها. سأصحّح إجاباتك اعتمادًا على الملخص، وأعرض مفتاح الإجابات في النهاية.

---

**1. (تذكّر)**
اكتب العلاقة التي تعطي المسافة التي تقطعها كلٌّ من العجلة اليسرى والعجلة اليمنى على الأرض في خطوة زمنية واحدة، واذكر معنى كل رمز فيها.

**2. (تذكّر)**
ما المصادر المنهجية الثلاثة لخطأ قياس المسافة بالعجلات؟ وما المصدر غير المنهجي الذي يذكره الملخص؟

**3. (فهم)**
في تحديث الوضعية تُستخدم الزاوية theta + dtheta/2 وليس theta. لماذا؟

**4. (فهم)**
يصف الملخص خطأ قياس المسافة بأنه "غير محدود". ماذا يعني ذلك؟ ولماذا يكبر خطأ الموضع كلما زادت المسافة المقطوعة؟

**5. (تطبيق)**
`from outside the material:` في خطوة زمنية واحدة قطعت العجلة اليمنى dS_R = 0.3 m والعجلة اليسرى dS_L = 0.1 m، وطول قاعدة العجلات L = 0.4 m.
احسب المسافة dS التي قطعها مركز الروبوت، وتغيّر الاتجاه dtheta.

**6. (تطبيق)**
`from outside the material:` في تقدير اتجاه روبوت خطأ مقداره 0.02 راديان. كم يقارب خطأ الموضع الناتج عن هذا الخطأ وحده بعد أن يقطع الروبوت 50 m إضافية؟ وما الفقرة من الملخص التي استندت إليها؟

# اختبار: المحاضرة 4، قياس المسافة بالعجلات

أجب عن الأسئلة الستة وأرسل إجاباتك، وسأصححها وأشرح لك أي خطأ.

---

**السؤال 1 (حساب)**
روبوت ذو دفع تفاضلي فيه r = 0.05 م و L = 0.5 م. دارت العجلة اليسرى dphi_L = 2 راديان، ودارت العجلة اليمنى dphi_R = 3 راديان في خطوة زمنية واحدة. احسب:
- أ) dS_L و dS_R
- ب) dS
- ج) dtheta

---

**السؤال 2 (مفهوم)**
عند تحديث الوضعية نستخدم الزاوية theta + dtheta/2 بدلًا من theta. لماذا؟

---

**السؤال 3 (حالات خاصة)**
- أ) إذا كان dS_R = dS_L، فكم تساوي dtheta؟ وكيف يتحرك الروبوت؟
- ب) إذا كان dS_R = −dS_L، فكم تساوي dS؟ وكيف يتحرك الروبوت؟

---

**السؤال 4 (حساب + تفسير)**
لدى الروبوت خطأ اتجاه قدره 0.02 راديان، ثم يسير مسافة إضافية قدرها 5 أمتار.
- أ) كم يبلغ خطأ الموضع تقريبًا؟
- ب) لماذا يُعدّ خطأ الاتجاه أسوأ أنواع الخطأ؟

---

**السؤال 5 (صح أم خطأ، مع التعليل)**
"خطأ قياس المسافة بالعجلات محدود، لأن الأخطاء الصغيرة في الخطوات المختلفة يلغي بعضها بعضًا مع الوقت."

---

**السؤال 6 (تصنيف)**
صنّف كل مصدر من مصادر الخطأ التالية إلى **منهجي** أو **غير منهجي**، ثم اذكر أيها لا يمكن معايرته:
- أ) انزلاق العجلات على أرضية مبتلة
- ب) قيمة L المستخدمة في البرنامج أكبر قليلًا من القيمة الحقيقية
- ج) نصف قطر العجلة اليمنى أكبر قليلًا من نصف قطر العجلة اليسرى
- د) المشفّر يعطي 360 نبضة فقط في الدورة الواحدة

---

# اختبار: المحاضرة 4 – قياس المسافة بالعجلات

أجب عن الأسئلة الستة، وبعدها أصحح إجاباتك وأشرح لك ما فاتك.

**السؤال 1 (مفاهيمي)**
في الدفع التفاضلي، ما الذي يمثله الرمز L، ولماذا يظهر في معادلة تغير الاتجاه dtheta ولا يظهر في معادلة المسافة dS؟

**السؤال 2 (حسابي)**
روبوت نصف قطر عجلتيه r = 0.05 m وقاعدة عجلاته L = 0.4 m. خلال خطوة زمنية واحدة دارت العجلة اليسرى بزاوية dphi_L = 10 rad، ودارت اليمنى بزاوية dphi_R = 12 rad.
احسب:
- أ) dS_L و dS_R
- ب) dS
- ج) dtheta، وحدد اتجاه دوران الروبوت (يسارًا أم يمينًا)

**السؤال 3 (تعليل)**
لماذا نستخدم theta + dtheta/2 لتحديث الموضع بدلًا من theta؟ وفي أي حالة يصبح الفرق بين الطريقتين صفرًا؟

**السؤال 4 (حسابي/تطبيقي)**
تراكم في تقدير اتجاه الروبوت خطأ مقداره 0.02 rad. ما الخطأ التقريبي في الموضع بعد أن يقطع الروبوت 50 m إضافية؟ ولماذا يعدّ خطأ الاتجاه أخطر من خطأ المسافة؟

**السؤال 5 (صح أم خطأ، مع التعليل)**
"إذا عايرنا الروبوت معايرة دقيقة جدًا فسيبقى خطأ قياس المسافة بالعجلات محدودًا مهما طالت المسافة."

**السؤال 6 (تصنيف)**
صنّف كل مصدر مما يلي إلى منهجي أو غير منهجي:
- أ) العجلة اليمنى أكبر قليلًا من اليسرى
- ب) الروبوت يسير على أرضية مبللة فتنزلق عجلاته
- ج) المشفّر يعطي 512 نبضة فقط في الدورة
- د) قيمة L المسجلة في البرنامج 0.40 m، لكن القيمة الحقيقية 0.41 m

ثم اشرح لماذا لا يمكن معايرة المصدر غير المنهجي.

- ج) dtheta، وحدد اتجاه دوران الروبوت (يسارًا أم يمينًا)

**السؤال 3 (تعليل)**
لماذا نستخدم theta + dtheta/2 لتحديث الموضع بدلًا من theta؟ وفي أي حالة يصبح الفرق بين الطريقتين صفرًا؟

**السؤال 4 (حسابي/تطبيقي)**
تراكم في تقدير اتجاه الروبوت خطأ مقداره 0.02 rad. ما الخطأ التقريبي في الموضع بعد أن يقطع الروبوت 50 m إضافية؟ ولماذا يعدّ خطأ الاتجاه أخطر من خطأ المسافة؟

**السؤال 5 (صح أم خطأ، مع التعليل)**
"إذا عايرنا الروبوت معايرة دقيقة جدًا فسيبقى خطأ قياس المسافة بالعجلات محدودًا مهما طالت المسافة."

**السؤال 6 (تصنيف)**
صنّف كل مصدر مما يلي إلى منهجي أو غير منهجي:
- أ) العجلة اليمنى أكبر قليلًا من اليسرى
- ب) الروبوت يسير على أرضية مبللة فتنزلق عجلاته
- ج) المشفّر يعطي 512 نبضة فقط في الدورة
- د) قيمة L المسجلة في البرنامج 0.40 m، لكن القيمة الحقيقية 0.41 m

ثم اشرح لماذا لا يمكن معايرة المصدر غير المنهجي.

**السؤال 1 (حسابي)**
لروبوت ذي دفع تفاضلي نصف قطر عجلته r = 0.05 م وقاعدة عجلاته L = 0.5 م. في خطوة زمنية واحدة دارت العجلة اليسرى dphi_L = 2 راديان ودارت اليمنى dphi_R = 4 راديان.
احسب: dS_L و dS_R و dS و dtheta.

**السؤال 2 (مفاهيمي)**
لماذا نستخدم الزاوية theta + dtheta/2 عند تحديث الموضع بدلًا من الزاوية theta في بداية الخطوة؟

**السؤال 3 (تطبيق)**
تراكم في تقدير اتجاه الروبوت خطأ مقداره 0.02 راديان. ما خطأ الموضع التقريبي الذي ينتج عن هذا الخطأ وحده بعد أن يقطع الروبوت 50 مترًا إضافية؟

ماذا يعني أن خطأ قياس المسافة بالعجلات "غير محدود"؟ ولماذا يكون كذلك؟

Prompt

اختبرني في هذا الملخص، 6 أسئلة.

المحاضرة 4: قياس المسافة بالعجلات (ملخص)

4.1 الدفع التفاضلي. للروبوت ذي الدفع التفاضلي عجلتان مدفوعتان نصف قطر كل منهما r على محور طوله L (قاعدة العجلات). إذا دارت العجلة اليسرى واليمنى بزاويتي dphi_L و dphi_R في خطوة زمنية واحدة، تقطع العجلتان على الأرض dS_L = r * dphi_L و dS_R = r * dphi_R.

4.2 تحديث الوضعية. المسافة التي يقطعها مركز الروبوت هي dS = (dS_R + dS_L) / 2 وتغير الاتجاه هو dtheta = (dS_R - dS_L) / L. يُستخدم نصف الزاوية theta + dtheta/2 لأن الروبوت يدور أثناء الخطوة، فيكون متوسط الاتجاه خلال الخطوة تقديرًا أفضل من الاتجاه في بدايتها.

4.3 نمو الخطأ. خطأ قياس المسافة غير محدود: كل خطوة تضيف خطأً لا يُزال أبدًا، لذلك ينمو خطأ الموضع مع المسافة المقطوعة. خطأ الاتجاه هو الأسوأ، لأن خطأ اتجاه مقداره e راديان يصبح خطأ موضع يقارب d * e بعد مسافة إضافية d.

4.4 مصادر الخطأ. ثلاثة مصادر منهجية: نصفا قطر غير متساويين للعجلتين، قاعدة عجلات L غير دقيقة، ودقة محدودة للمشفرات. انزلاق العجلات مصدر غير منهجي؛ لا يمكن معايرته.

The material (Arabic lecture notes on wheel odometry) says: wheel travel dS_L = rdphi_L and dS_R = rdphi_R; dS = (dS_R+dS_L)/2; dtheta = (dS_R-dS_L)/L; the half angle theta+dtheta/2 is used because the robot turns during the step; odometry error is unbounded and grows with distance; a heading error of e radians becomes a position error of about d*e after a further distance d; three systematic error sources (unequal wheel radii, inaccurate L, finite encoder resolution); wheel slip is non-systematic and cannot be calibrated out.
